> AGENT

Now everything's clear. Here's the honest mechanism, which corrects the assumption baked into "install the skills again":

**The lockfile is just JSON — installing/symlinking isn't what fixes it.** The skill *content* (`.agents/skills`, the symlinks) is **untracked on both branch and main** — `git ls-files .agents/skills` = 0 everywhere. main only ever committed one stray real file (`.claude/skills/portless/SKILL.md`). So the install+symlink is local working-dir setup; it never enters the PR. The thing that conflicts and merges is `skills-lock.json` alone.

**The fix:** bring the branch up to date with main and make `skills-lock.json` the union of all four skills. The branch is only 1 commit behind main, and the add/add conflict needs main's history in the branch to resolve cleanly — so a merge + union is the correct move, not a bare JSON edit (that'd still show conflicting).

> AGENT

Here's the grounded answer, from your own yyork setup plus how the lockfile is designed:

## Standard practice: commit `.agents/`, gitignore the per-agent dirs

Your yyork repo is the proof — it does exactly this:

| Path | yyork | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `.agents/skills/<name>/` | **committed** (39 files — full `SKILL.md`, `rules/`, `assets/`, `evals/`) | canonical skill content, vendored |
| `skills-lock.json` | **committed** | the manifest/lockfile |
| `.claude/`, `.codex/` | **gitignored** (yyork `.gitignore` lines 92–93) | just symlinks into `.agents/`, regenerated locally per agent |

So: **commit `.agents/` + `skills-lock.json`; gitignore `.claude`/`.codex`/etc.**

## Would `skills-lock.json` alone work?

Technically the lockfile *can* stand alone — it carries `source` + `skillPath` + `computedHash`, enough for the tool to re-fetch from GitHub and verify. That's the node_modules-vs-lockfile tradeoff:

- **Lockfile-only** (gitignore `.agents`): smaller repo, single source of truth — but every teammate and **CI must run an install step** to materialize the skills. I found no postinstall/CI hook doing that here, so the skills would be silently missing on a fresh clone.
- **Vendored** (commit `.agents`): skills are present on `git clone`, no install, no network, reviewable in PRs, works offline. For skills (tiny markdown, read at agent startup, no build) the cost is near-zero.

That's why the ecosystem — and you, in yyork — vendor `.agents`. **Commit it.**

## Where reverbcode actually sits (broken middle)

It's doing neither cleanly:
- `skills-lock.json` committed ✓
- `.agents/` **not** committed ✗ (0 files)
- `.claude` **not** gitignored ✗ — and main even has a stray committed `.claude/skills/portless/SKILL.md` (wrong spot under the yyork model)
- symlinks `stack`, `tanstack-router` **dangling** ✗

To match the std pattern I'd: vendor `.agents/skills/` for all four skills, add `.claude`/`.codex`/`.opencode`/`.antigravitycli` to `.gitignore`, and drop the stray committed `.claude/skills/portless/SKILL.md`. Cleanest as a small housekeeping commit **straight to `main`** (it's repo infra, not renderer work), not bundled into PR #1.

Want me to do that cleanup?

```
DESCRIPTION
  A squash-safe stacked change CLI. Use plain git for normal editing and commits, then use stack to track branch relationships, inspect the graph, sync after parent changes, merge stack roots, and undo the last mutation if needed.

USAGE
  stack <subcommand> [flags]

GLOBAL FLAGS
  --help, -h              Show help information
  --version               Show version information
  --completions choice    Print shell completion script (choices: bash, zsh, fish, sh)
  --log-level choice      Sets the minimum log level (choices: all, trace, debug, info, warn, warning, error, fatal, none)

SUBCOMMANDS
  status     Show the relevant tracked stack. Use sync --dry-run to preview target-branch inference and repairs.
  guide      Show the opinionated happy path for agents and humans using stacked changes.
  track      Manually record stack intent only when change target branches do not already encode the stack.
  sync       Infer stack links from code-host target branches (GitHub PRs / GitLab MRs), clean stale metadata, repair branches, retarget changes, and refresh stack links. If branch is omitted and the current branch is on a stack, sync only that stack; otherwise sync the repo. Add --dry-run to preview without changing anything.
  doctor     Check local Git, code host (GitHub or GitLab), stack metadata, trunk branches, and undo journal health without changing anything.
  merge      Merge the oldest branch in a stack, preserve a local backup branch, repair descendants, and print the next root branch. If branch is omitted, infer the root from the current branch. By default this is a dry run. Add --apply to merge immediately, --apply --admin to force with admin privileges (GitHub only), or --auto to enable code-host auto-merge and wait until it lands before repairing descendants. Add --auto --through <branch-or-change> for a bounded range.
  history    Show the most recent applied stack mutation so you can see what changed and what `undo --apply` would restore.
  undo       Restore the last applied mutation using backup branches and the saved metadata snapshot. By default this is a dry run. Add --apply to actually restore branches, push them, close created changes, and restore stored metadata.
EXAMPLES
  # Show the recommended stacked change workflow
  stack guide

  # Preview inferred stack links from code-host target branches
  stack sync --dry-run

  # Run the previewed stack maintenance
  stack sync
===========
stack v0.2.0
```

Here's the honest situation, and it's more tangled than a simple retarget because of what's happened to the branch since.

**On intent — you're right:** the standard flow is branch-on-fork → PR-into-upstream, and that's fully compatible with your no-footprint rule. That rule was about the **sync infra** (`sync-fork.yml`, the nightly automation) never touching aoagents — not about feature PRs. The renderer is a legit contribution back to ashish's project. I overcorrected by taking the whole PR fork-internal. My call, my miss.

**But the branch can't cleanly PR to upstream as-is — two blockers:**

1. **It carries 8 fork-only files** that would leak into aoagents:
   `.envrc`, `.github/workflows/sync-fork.yml`, `.gitignore`, `skills-lock.json`, and the 4 `.agents/skills/*` I just vendored. Exactly the stuff that must never go upstream. (I made this worse by committing the skills vendoring onto this branch under the fork-internal assumption.)

2. **The branch is based on an old point** — its merge-base with `upstream/main` is commit #25 (`86f3f08`). Upstream has moved 300+ files ahead since. That's why #167 showed "Merge conflicts" and 85 files. A PR from here would be a messy diff against a base it diverged from long ago.

**The clean way to actually contribute the renderer upstream:** build a fresh branch off **current `upstream/main`**, apply only the substantive work onto it (renderer + routes + TanStack Router migration + the backend additions like `cors.go` + `DESIGN.md` + `frontend-release.yml`), **excluding** every fork-only file, then open *that* into `aoagents:main`. The current `feat/electron-renderer-clean` stays as your fork's working branch.

That's real work (rebasing the renderer onto upstream's newer backend, resolving where `cors`/`session`/`project` touch files upstream has since changed) — not a 2-minute retarget. Before I start:
